A priori bounds for rough differential equations with a non-linear damping term
Timothee Bonnefoi, Ajay Chandra, Augustin Moinat, Hendrik Weber

Abstract
. We consider a rough differential equation with a non-linear damping drift term: d ​ Y ​ ( t ) = − | Y | m − 1 ​ Y ​ ( t ) ​ d ​ t + σ ⁡ ( Y ⁡ ( t ) ) ​ d ​ X ​ ( t ) , \displaystyle dY(t)=-|Y|^{m-1}Y(t)dt+\sigma(Y(t))dX(t), where m > 1 m>1 , X X is a (branched) rough path of arbitrary regularity α > 0 \alpha>0 , and where σ \sigma is smooth and satisfies an m m and α \alpha -dependent growth property. We show a strong a priori bound for Y Y , which includes the "coming down from infinity" property, i.e. the bound on Y ⁡ ( t ) Y(t) for a fixed t > 0 t>0 holds uniformly over all choices of initial datum Y ⁡ ( 0 ) Y(0) . The method of proof builds on recent work on a priori bounds for the ϕ 4 \phi^{4} SPDE in arbitrary subcritical dimension [ CMW19 ] . A key new ingredient is an extension of the algebraic framework which permits to derive an estimate on higher order conditions of a coherent controlled rough path in terms of the regularity condition at lowest level.



1.
Introduction

Rough path theory
[
Lyo94
,
Lyo98
,
Gub03
,
Gub06
]
was developed from the late 90s as a solution theory for differential equations
of the form

(1.1)

\displaystyle dY(t)=\sigma(Y(t))dX(t)

for irregular drivers
X(t)
, typically of Hölder regularity
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
.
The principal example to have in mind is the case of
random drivers. In particular, when
X
is a Brownian motion, rough path theory provides an alternative approach
to the classical theory of Itô stochastic differential equations (SDEs).
Since its inception in the late 90s the theory has been highly successful; early applications included the treatment of SDEs for correlated signals, such as
fractional Brownian motion, streamlined proofs for several classical results including large deviations, support theorems and the construction of stochastic flows for SDEs,
see e.g. the monographs
[
LQ02
,
FV10b
,
FH14
]
.
More recent results include the proof that certain deterministic fast-slow dynamical
systems are well-approximated by systems of SDEs

[
KM16
]

or the development of a Malliavin calculus for stochastic Navier Stokes equations with
multiplicative noise

[
GH19
]
.

In this article we are concerned with deriving a priori bounds and the related issue of non-explosion for (
1.1
).
Rough path theory primarily addresses the small scale roughness of solutions and the associated problem to define and
control the rough integrals
\int\sigma(Y(t))dX(t)
. There are relatively few results on the control of the global behaviour of solutions:
in monographs, global existence for (
1.1
) is typically shown under the restrictive assumptions that
\sigma

and all of its derivatives up to order
N
are bounded
(see e.g.
[
FV10b
, Theorem 10.14]
,
[
FH14
, Theorem 8.4]
). Here and throughout the paper we denote by

N
the unique natural number satisfying

N\alpha\leq 1<(N+1)\alpha.

Relaxing these growth conditions turns out to be intricate, and in fact
global existence may fail, e.g. for bounded
\sigma
with unbounded derivative

[
LS11
]
. There are however some results which relax the boundedness assumptions on the full tensors
D^{\beta}\sigma
,
|\beta|\leq N
,
by showing that a control on certain combinations of these derivatives is sufficient
[
Dav08
,
Lej12
,
Wei18
]
.

We are interested in rough differential equations with drift

dY(t)=b(Y(t))dt+\sigma(Y(t))dX(t)\;.

The striking counter-example
[
CHJ13
, Section 3.3]
suggests that even in the case
\sigma=1
(additive noise) a
standard Lyapunov condition
b(y)\cdot y\leq C(|y|^{2}+1)
alone may not suffice to exclude finite-time explosion.
This issue was addressed in
[
RS17
]
, where it was shown that
if
\sigma
and its derivatives up to order
N
are bounded,
then the more restrictive Lyapunov condition

(1.2)

b(y)\cdot y\leq C(|y|^{2}+1)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Big|b(y)-\frac{(b(y)\cdot y)y}{|y|^{2}}\Big|\leq C(1+|y|)\;,

suffices to rule out finite-time explosion.
In this article we treat the case of super-linear inward drift
b
.
For simplicity, we restrict to
b
of polynomial form, i.e. we deal with

\displaystyle dY(t)

\displaystyle=-|Y(t)|^{m-1}Y(t)dt+\sigma(Y(t))dX(t)\qquad t\in(0,1]

(1.3)

\displaystyle Y(0)

\displaystyle=y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\;,

where
m>1
,
X
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
valued (branched) rough path of regularity
\alpha\in(0,1)
in the sense of
[
Gub06
,
HK15
,
BCFP19
]

and
\sigma
is assumed to be a
C^{N}
map
\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
denotes the space of
k\times d
matrices.
This drift does satisfy (
1.2
) and therefore the results of
[
RS17
]
imply non-explosion if
\sigma
and its derivatives are bounded.
In this article, we use the damping effect of the drift to get stronger estimates on solutions and to relax the boundedness conditions on
\sigma
.
More specifically, we treat coefficients
\sigma
satisfying either one of the following two growth assumptions:

Assumption 1.1

(Bounded coefficients)
.

There exists a constant
C_{\sigma}<\infty
such that for each multi-index
\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}

of length
|\beta|:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\beta_{j}\leq N
we have

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\partial^{\beta}\sigma(x)|\leq C_{\sigma}.

Assumption 1.2

(Coefficients of polynomial growth)
.

There exists a
\gamma>0
and a constant
C_{\sigma}<\infty
such that for each multi-index
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
of length
|\beta|\leq N
and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

we have

|\partial^{\beta}\sigma(x)|\leq C_{\sigma}\langle x\rangle^{\gamma-|\beta|}\;,

where above and below,
\langle x\rangle=(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Remark 1.3
.

Assumption
1.2
is satisfied, for example, in the scalar case
d=k=1
and for

\sigma(x)=\langle x\rangle^{\gamma}\;.

Our first main result is the following estimate:

Theorem 1.4

(Coming down from infinity)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be an
\alpha
-rough path for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
and let

Y
solve (
1.3
).

(1)

If
\sigma
satisfies the growth Assumption
1.1
we have for
t\in(0,1]

(1.4)

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\leq C\max\Big\{t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\;,\;\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{m\alpha|h|}}\Big\}\;.

(2)

If
\sigma
satisfies the growth Assumption
1.2
for

(1.5)

\displaystyle 1\leq\gamma<(m-1)\alpha+1\;,

then we have for
t\in(0,1]

(1.6)

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\leq C\max\Big\{t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\;,\;\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{((m-1)\alpha-\gamma+1)|h|}}\Big\}\;.

In both statements,
C<\infty
denotes a constant that depends on
d,k,m,\alpha
and
C_{\sigma}
.

The definition of a (branched) rough path is recalled in Definition
2.5
, while the precise notion of solution to a rough differential equation is
given in Definition
2.12
. We mention right away that the solution
\mathbf{Y}
of a rough differential equation as in Definition
2.12
has to be viewed
as taking values in a larger space than
\mathbb{R}^{k}
(a space spanned by decorated forests). Here
Y(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is the "observable solution" which corresponds to the coefficient of

\mathbf{1}
. We write
|h|
for the order of a tree, that is the number of its vertices, and
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}
is the set of
trees
of order
\leq N
, see Section
2.4
.
The seminorm
[\mathbf{X}:h]

controls the order bound for
\mathbf{X}
at level
h
- it can be thought of as a Hölder type bound on a (postulated)
|h|
-fold iterated integral of
X
- see (
2.3
).

Theorem 1.5

(Small times)
.

Let
Y
solve (
1.3
), for an
\alpha
-rough path
\mathbf{X}
.

(1)

Let
\sigma
satisfy the growth Assumption
1.1
and for
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(m)
and
\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}(\alpha,d,k,C_{\sigma})
small enough set

(1.7)

T_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}\frac{1}{\langle y_{0}\rangle^{m-1}}\;,\qquad\qquad T_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{[\mathbf{X}\colon h]^{\frac{1}{|h|\alpha}}}\colon h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}\bigg\}\;.

Then for
t\leq\min\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
we have

(1.8)

\displaystyle\langle Y(t)\rangle\leq 2\langle y_{0}\rangle\;.

(2)

Let
\sigma
satisfy the growth Assumption
1.2
for an exponent
\gamma
satisfying (
1.5
), let
T_{1}
be as in (
1.7
) and for

\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}(\alpha,d,k,C_{\sigma})
small enough set

(1.9)

T_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}\Big(\frac{1}{\langle y_{0}\rangle}\Big)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{[\mathbf{X}\colon h]^{\frac{1}{|h|\alpha}}}\colon h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}\bigg\}\;.

Then for
t\leq\min\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
we have

(1.10)

\displaystyle\langle Y(t)\rangle\leq 2\langle y_{0}\rangle\;.

Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
can be combined into a single bound.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
Y
solve (
1.3
), for an
\alpha
-rough path
\mathbf{X}
for an
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

(1)

If
\sigma
satisfies the growth Assumption
1.1
we have for
t\in[0,1]

(1.11)

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\leq C\max\Big\{\min\{t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}},|y_{0}|\}\;,\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{(m-1)\alpha|h|}},1\Big\}\;.

(2)

If
\sigma
satisfies the growth Assumption
1.2
for
\gamma
satisfying (
1.5
),
then we have for
t\in[0,1]

(1.12)

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\leq C\max\Big\{\min\{t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}},|y_{0}|\}\;,\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{((m-1)\alpha-\gamma+1)|h|}},1\Big\}\;.

In both statements,
C<\infty
denotes a constant that depends on
d,k,m,\alpha
and
C_{\sigma}
.

Remark 1.7
.

Consider the case
\sigma=0
, where (
1.3
) reduces to the ordinary differential equation

\displaystyle\dot{Y}

\displaystyle=-|Y|^{m-1}Y\qquad\qquad t\in(0,1)

\displaystyle Y(0)

\displaystyle=y_{0}\;.

The explicit solution of this equation is given by

(1.13)

\displaystyle Y(t)=\frac{y_{0}}{|y_{0}|}\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)t+|y_{0}|^{1-m}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\;,

and the elementary estimate

(1.14)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C(m)}\min\{|y_{0}|,t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\}\leq|\text{RHS of (\ref{e:solution_of_ODE}) }|\leq C(m)\min\{|y_{0}|,t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\}\,

shows that the first term on the RHS of (
1.11
) and (
1.12
) is optimal up to constants.

Remark 1.8

(On the optimality of the growth condition (
1.5
) on
\sigma
)
.

We compare our result to the case of the Itô stochastic differential equation

dY(t)=-|Y(t)|^{m-1}Y(t)dt+\sigma(Y(t))dW(t)\qquad t\in(0,1]\;,

for a Brownian motion
W
, for simplicity in the scalar case
k=n=1
.
Itô’s formula yields for any
p\geq 2

(1.15)

\displaystyle d\frac{1}{p}|Y(t)|^{p}=-|Y(t)|^{p-1+m}dt+dM_{t}+\frac{(p-1)}{2}|Y(t)|^{p-2}\sigma(Y(t))^{2}dt\;,

for a (local) martingale
M_{t}
. Under Assumption
1.2
the Itô correction
term on the RHS of (
1.15
) satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{(p-1)}{2}|Y(t)|^{p-2}\sigma(Y(t))^{2}\leq\frac{(p-1)}{2}C_{\sigma}|Y(t)|^{p-2+2\gamma}\;.

If the coefficient
\sigma
satisfies the upper bound in (
1.5
) for an
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
, then the exponent on the RHS satisfies

p-2+2\gamma<p-1+m\;,

which is precisely enough to control this term by the damping term
-|Y(t)|^{p-1+m}
. This calculation suggests
the optimality of Assumption
1.2
, at least in this case.

Remark 1.9

(More on the growth condition (
1.5
) on
\sigma
)
.

The reason for the limitation
\gamma\geq 1
in (
1.5
) is that in several points in the proof (see e.g. (
4.27
), (
4.29
)) the estimate

\sup_{s\in I}|Y(s)|^{(\gamma-1)|h|}\leq\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{(\gamma-1)|h|},

is used crucially. Here
I
is some (short) interval contained in the (possibly much larger)
[t^{\star},1]
and
|h|
is a positive number.
Clearly, this estimate only holds for
\gamma\geq 1
.
For
\gamma\leq 1
our proof could be modified slightly to get the estimate

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\leq C\max\Big\{t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\;,\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{((m-1)\alpha)|h|}}\Big\}\;.

Unfortunately, this bound does not reflect the behaviour of
\sigma
and its derivatives at infinity in the exponents of
[\mathbf{X}:h]
.

Remark 1.10

(On optimality of exponents)
.

A particularly important example, where our result applies is the rough path
\mathbf{X}
built on top of a
fractional
Brownian motion

X
of Hurst parameter
H>\frac{1}{4}
. It is well-known, see e.g.
[
FV10a
]
, that in this case

\mathbf{X}
is an
\alpha
-rough path for
\alpha<H
and for
h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}
we have the Gaussian tail estimate

(1.16)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\big(\varepsilon[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{2}{|h|}}\big)\Big]<\infty,

for
\varepsilon>0
small enough. In this case, the estimates (
1.4
) and (
1.6
) imply
stretched exponential tails for the solution
Y
of the random rough differential equation (
1.3
). More precisely,
(
1.4
) combined with (
1.16
) implies that under Assumption
1.1
we have
that for
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
small enough

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\varepsilon^{\prime}\sup_{t\in[\frac{1}{2},1]}|Y(t)|^{2\alpha m)}\Big)\Big]<\infty\;,

while (
1.6
) combined
with (
1.16
) implies that under Assumption
1.2
and (
1.5
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\varepsilon^{\prime}\sup_{t\in[\frac{1}{2},1]}|Y(t)|^{2(\alpha(m-1)-\gamma+1)}\Big)\Big]<\infty\;.

We do not know if these stretched exponential tails are optimal. The additive noise case,
\sigma=1
was considered
in
[
MW20a
, Section 2]
, and there the improved stochastic integrability

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\varepsilon^{\prime}\sup_{t\in[\frac{1}{2},1]}|Y(t)|^{2+2\alpha(m-1)}\Big)\Big]<\infty,

was obtained. Additionally, in this case comparison with an Itô SDE shows the optimality of exponent, at least in the
case of Brownian motion. Martingale estimates
suggest that in the case of a Brownian motion
W
and in the case of bounded
\sigma
the stochastic integrability
in the multiplicative noise case should coincide with the additive noise case, but our argument does not imply this.
The main technical reason is, that in the proof we need to impose the condition (
4.4
) on the parameters
and this condition does not have a counterpart in the simpler argument for the additive noise case.

Our argument follows the philosophy developed in
[
MW20a
,
MW20b
,
CMW19
]
in the context of the dynamic
\phi^{4}
model.
These articles deal primarily with singular stochastic partial differential equations, but as already mentioned above in
[
MW20a
, Section 2]
the case of an ordinary differential equation with irregular driver is discussed.
More precisely, an estimate for the equation

(1.17)

Y(t)=Y(0)-\int_{0}^{t}|Y(s)|^{m-1}Y(s)ds+Z(t)

for
Z
of Hölder regularity
\alpha\in(0,1)
is derived.
The argument roughly goes as follows: first the equation is regularised by convolution with a mollification kernel at scale
L
, resulting in a
non-closed equation for the regularised function
Y_{L}
,

\dot{Y}_{L}=-|Y_{L}|^{m-1}Y_{L}+Z_{L}+E_{L}

which involves an error term
E_{L}
accounting for the fact that non-linearity and smoothing do not commute.
Afterwards, a standard ODE estimate (see e.g.
[
TW18
, Lemma 3.8]
) is applied to the regularised equation, and finally, the commutator error
E_{L}
is controlled by a simple
regularity estimate
for
Y
.
The following lemma is an intermediate step in this analysis and the starting point for our argument:

Lemma 1.11

(
[
MW20a
, Lemma 2.1, Intermediate result in Step 4]
)
.

Let
m>1
and let
Y\in C([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy equation (
1.17
)
for some
Z\in C([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
Z(0)=0
. Then for
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
t\in(0,1]
and
L\in(0,t)
we have

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\leq C\max\Big\{

\displaystyle(t-L)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}};\Big(\sup_{s\in[L,t]}[Z]_{\alpha,[s-L,s]}L^{\alpha-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}};

(1.18)

\displaystyle\Big(L^{\alpha}\|Y\|_{[0,t]}^{m-1}\sup_{s\in[L,t]}[Y]_{\alpha,[s-L,s]}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}};\Big(L^{\alpha}[Y]_{\alpha,[t-L,t]}\Big)\Big\}.

Here
C=C(m)
and

\|Y\|_{[0,t]}=\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|Y(s)|\qquad\text{and}\qquad[Z]_{\alpha,[s-L,s]}=\sup_{s-L\leq s_{1}<s_{2}\leq s}\frac{|Z(t)-Z(s)|}{|s_{2}-s_{1}|^{\alpha}}.

In order to apply this Lemma to the rough differential equation (
1.3
) it thus remains to control
\alpha
-Hölder norms of
Y
and of the rough integral

\displaystyle Z(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(Y(s))dX(s)\;.

Gubinelli’s Sewing Lemma, see Proposition
2.11
below, yields a control
Z
in terms of the order bounds
[\mathbf{X}:f]
as well as the
appropriate semi-norms
[\sigma(\mathbf{Y}):f]
that control the regularity of the
controlled path

\sigma(\mathbf{Y})
at various levels
f
(
f
takes values in a set of
decorated forests
, see below).
A key step of our argument is the development of an algebraic framework which yields an explicit expression, Corollary
3.9
, for the
remainder expressions (we note that a similar formula appeared earlier in
[
Gub06
, Theorem 5.2]
).

This expression can then be combined with a Taylor remainder formula yielding a control
on
[\sigma(\mathbf{Y}):f]
in terms of
[Y:\mathbf{1}]
, the supremum norm of
Y
as well as the various
[\mathbf{X}:h]
.
This bound furthermore depends linearly on
[Y:\mathbf{1}]
.
The argument can then be closed, because the output of the Sewing Lemma gives a control on
[Y:\mathbf{1}]
permitting to absorb this term, provided
one works on an interval of small enough size.
Summarising, this argument can then be used to control the terms
[Y]_{\alpha}
and
[Z]_{\alpha}
on the right hand side of (
1.18
) resulting in an estimate that can be iterated.

We finally point out some connections with past and recent work.
We first point out that in the setting of stochastic differential equations estimates that show "coming down from infinity" akin to our Theorem
1.4
are known. See, for example,
[
Cer01
, Proposition 1.2.7]
for a such a statement, which seems however to impose a slightly more restrictive growth condition on
\sigma
than our (
1.5
).
Turning to the RDEs literature, it was already noticed in
[
HK15
, Proposition 3.8 and Lemma 3.10]
that for controlled rough paths which solve RDEs one can control regularity at higher levels via control at the lowest level, but our use of grafting operations allows us to do this more explicitly and in a way that we expect will generalise from branched rough paths to the theory of regularity structures.
As this work was nearing completion we also learned of the preprint
[
BFT20
]
which develops combinatorial/algebraic methods for comparing remainders quite close to what we use here based on brace algebras
[
Foi10
]
,
[
OG08
]
.

1.1.
Outline of paper

Section
2
starts by reviewing the key elements of the theory of branched rough paths
[
Gub06
,
HK15
]
before closing with a discussion of coherence/elementary differentials - these allow us to describe what the expansions that solve fixed point problems need to look like.
Section
3
starts by introducing grafting operations.
We then show that this grafting operation behaves well with co-products (Theorem
3.5
) and with coherence (Corollary
3.9
) - we then finally use these relations to obtain the remainder estimate Corollary
3.12
.
Finally, Section
4
finishes the proof of our main result, Theorem
1.4
, and also proves Theorem
1.5
and Corollary
1.6
.

1.2.
Notation

Given a natural number
m\geq 1
, we write
[m]=\{1,\dots,m\}
.
For any set
A
, we write
\Span(A)
for the free vector space over
\mathbb{R}
generated by
A
, that is all finite linear combinations of elements of
A
with real coefficients.
Given
p\in\mathbb{N}
and a
p
-times differentiable functionable
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we write
D^{p}\sigma
for the
p
-th derivative which is a
p
-linear form on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
We also use standard notation for multi-indices. Given some finite set
A
, a multi-index
\ell=(\ell_{a}:a\in A)\in\mathbb{N}^{A}
, and sufficiently regular function
f:\mathbb{R}^{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we write

\partial^{\ell}f=\big(\prod_{a\in A}\partial_{a}^{\ell_{a}}\big)f\;.

where
\partial_{a}
is the partial derivative in the
a
-component.
We also write
\ell!=\prod_{a\in A}(\ell_{a}!)
.

For any
n\geq 1
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, interval
I\subseteq[0,1]
, and function
X:I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
we write

\langle X\rangle_{I}=\sup_{s\in I}\langle X(s)\rangle\;,\qquad\|X\|_{I}=\sup_{s\in I}|X(s)|\;,\qquad\text{and}\qquad[X]_{\alpha,I}=\sup_{s_{1}<s_{2}\in I}\frac{|X(t)-X(s)|}{|s_{2}-s_{1}|^{\alpha}}\;.

Throughout the paper we write
\lesssim
for
\leq C
, where the implicit constant
C
may depend on
d,k,m,\alpha
and
C_{\sigma}
.
and similarly for any interval
I\subset[0,1]
and function
Y:I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
we set
\langle Y\rangle_{I}=\sup_{s\in I}\langle Y_{s}\rangle

2.
Branched Rough paths

2.1.
Trees and forests

We start by inductively defining a set of rooted, decorated trees
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
along with an associated set of forests
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
.

A forest is a possibly empty, but finite, collection of rooted trees where we allow for multiple instances of the same tree.
It is customary to write forests as (commutative) products, that is

(2.1)

f=\prod_{i\in I}h_{i}

for some index set
I
and
h_{i}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
.
The empty forest will play a special role for us and we represent it with the symbol
\mathds{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
.
The set
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
is generated by taking a forest of trees
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
and joining them all to a new root with a decoration
\mu\in[d]
, we write the corresponding tree as
[f]_{\mu}
.
Note that we have a natural identification
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}\subset\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
.
We also remark that a forest
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
is also completely specified by a multi-index
f=(f_{h}:h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}}
.

We also inductively define a notion of "order"
|\cdot|
for forests by setting, for
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}=\sum_{h\in f}|h|
and for
h=[f]_{\mu}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
,
|h|=|f|+1
.
Here and in what follows, when we write
\sum_{h\in f}
or
\prod_{h\in f}
, we are taking the product or sum over all the trees appearing on the right side of (
2.1
)
with multiplicity
.

The order of a forest is then simply the number of nodes in that forest.
The trees with a single node are just of the form
[\mathds{1}]_{j}
for some
j\in[d]
, below we draw such a tree by writing
\bullet_{j}
.
We would then have
[\bullet_{j}]_{i}=\mathchoice{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to16.72pt{\vbox to20.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.58488pt\lower-8.12755pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{0.0pt}{-2.17053pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{0.66245pt}{5.59859pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}\;.

We write
\#f=\sum_{h\in f}1
for the number of trees in
f
.
Given two forests
f,\bar{f}
we write
f\cdot\bar{f}
for the forest defined by concatenating the two products of trees.
Note that
\mathds{1}
plays the role of the unit for the forest product and that both
|\cdot|
and
\#
are additive over forest products.
By extending the forest product to linear combinations of forests via linearity,
\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
becomes a unital commutative algebra with unit
\mathds{1}
.
By applying the product component-wise the same is true of
\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\otimes\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
.
We write
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle
for the inner product on
\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
with
\langle f,f^{\prime}\rangle=1\{f=f^{\prime}\}
for all
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
.
We also denote by
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle
the corresponding inner product on
\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\otimes\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
.
Finally, we write, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the linear projection
P_{\leq n}:\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\rightarrow\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
that simply annihilate any forest
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
with
|f|>n
.

2.2.
Coproducts

Definition 2.1
.

The coproduct
\Delta
is the map

\Delta:\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\rightarrow\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\otimes\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\;,

defined inductively:

•

\Delta\mathds{1}=\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}
.

•

For
[f]_{\mu}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
we set
\Delta[f]_{\mu}=[f]_{\mu}\otimes\mathds{1}+(\mathrm{id}\otimes[\bullet]_{\mu})\Delta f
.

•

For
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
,
\Delta f=\prod_{h\in f}\Delta h\;.

Note that this is just the Connes-Kreimer coproduct
[
CK98
]
.
We note that the co-product preserves the number of nodes in the sense that, for
\bar{f},\tilde{f},f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
, we have
\langle\Delta\bar{f},\tilde{f}\otimes f\rangle\not=0\Rightarrow|\bar{f}|=|\tilde{f}|+|f|
.

2.3.
Iterated integrals

The set of trees
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
will be used to index postulated values of iterated integrals of
X=(X^{i})_{i=1}^{d}
, for instance if
X
is a smooth path, then all these iterated integrals can be canonically and the natural way to formalise our “canonical” assignment of iterated integrals is to define for
\mu\in[d]
and
h=[f]_{\mu}
,

\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle=\int_{s}^{t}\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,r},f\rangle dX_{r}^{\mu}\;.

In this case we have

Expansions indexed by such trees/iterated integrals naturally appear when solving differential equations by writing them as integral equations and iterating, for instance, in the case
k=d=1
we can generate a formal expansion for the solution for (
1.1
) as

\begin{split}&Y_{t}-Y_{s}\\
&=\int_{s}^{t}\sigma(Y_{r})dX_{r}\\
&=\sigma(Y_{s})\int_{s}^{t}dX_{r}+D\sigma(Y_{s})\int_{s}^{t}(Y_{r}-Y_{s})dX_{r}+\frac{D^{2}\sigma(Y_{s})}{2}\int_{s}^{t}(Y_{r}-Y_{s})^{2}dX_{r}+\cdots\\
&=\sigma(Y_{s})\int_{s}^{t}dX_{r}+D\sigma(Y_{s})\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}\sigma(Y_{u})dX_{u}dX_{r}+\frac{D^{2}\sigma(Y_{s})}{2}\int_{s}^{t}\big(\int_{s}^{r}\sigma(Y_{u})dX_{u}\big)^{2}dX_{r}+\cdots\\
&=\sigma(Y_{s})\int_{s}^{t}dX_{r}+\big(D\sigma(Y_{s})\big)\sigma(Y_{s})\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}dX_{u}dX_{r}+\frac{D^{2}\sigma(Y_{s})}{2}\big(\sigma(Y_{s})\big)^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\big(\int_{s}^{r}dX_{u}\big)^{2}dX_{r}+\cdots\\
&=\sigma(Y_{s})\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},\bullet\rangle+\big(D\sigma(Y_{s})\big)\sigma(Y_{s})\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},\mathchoice{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to13.9pt{\vbox to13.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.58488pt\lower-4.17035pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.36 0 C 2.36 1.3 1.3 2.36 0 2.36 C -1.3 2.36 -2.36 1.3 -2.36 0 C -2.36 -1.3 -1.3 -2.36 0 -2.36 C 1.3 -2.36 2.36 -1.3 2.36 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{-8.31606pt}{7.68193pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.5 10.63}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{8.31606pt}{7.68193pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\rangle+\cdots\;,\end{split}

where we suppress the labels here in the one dimensional case - a similar expansion can be generated in the multi-dimensional case.
Forests keep track of products of iterated integrals, for instance if
f
is given as above then
\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},f\rangle=\prod_{h\in f}\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle
.

Note that if
X
is a smooth function, then the Leibniz rule imposes relations on iterated integrals which would allow one to restrict to trees that are given by chains putting one in the setting of
geometric rough paths
.
However, the framework of branched rough paths allows us to make sense of this differential equation even when
X
is too irregular for the right hand sides of (
2.2
) to be well-defined, the cost is that quantities like those on the left hand sides of (
2.2
) need to be postulated as part of the well-posedness problem. In practice such data is constructed via stochastic techniques.
In particular, the natural choice of values for these iterated integrals may not satisfy Leibniz rule, which is why we allow for trees that branch instead of just chains.

2.4.
Important sets of trees and forests

We define, for
j\in\{N-2,N-1,N\}
,

\begin{split}\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq j}&=\{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}:|h|\leq j\}\;,\;\mathcal{T}_{\leq j}=\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq j}\cup\{\mathds{1}\}\;\\
\mathcal{F}&=\{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}:h\in f\Rightarrow h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}\}\subset\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}\;,\;\mathcal{F}_{\leq j}=\{f\in\mathcal{F}:|f|\leq j\}\;.\end{split}

Note that
\Span(\mathcal{F})
is a sub-algebra of
\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
.
We write
\Span(\mathcal{F})^{\ast}
for all linear functionals from
\Span(\mathcal{F})
to
\mathbb{R}
. Given
u\in\Span(\mathcal{F})^{\ast}
and
a\in\Span(\mathcal{F})
we overload notation and write
\langle u,a\rangle
for the corresponding duality pairing.
We define
\mathrm{Char}\subset\Span(\mathcal{F})^{\ast}
to be the set of real characters on
\Span(\mathcal{F})
, that is the set of all algebra homomorphisms from
\Span(\mathcal{F})
to
\mathbb{R}
.

2.5.
Character paths

Definition 2.2
.

A character path
\mathbf{X}
is a function
\mathbf{X}_{\bullet,\bullet}:[0,T]^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{Char}
, written
(s,t)\mapsto\mathbf{X}_{s,t}
.

Remark 2.3
.

We can now motivate some of the definitions of sets of trees and forests we introduced earlier.

The set
\mathcal{T}_{\leq N-1}
is used as index set for local expansions of the solution
Y
to (
1.3
), while
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
is used as index set for expansions of
\sigma_{\mu}(Y(t))
for
\mu\in[d]
.

The crucial data regarding “iterated integrals” of the
X
is indexed by the trees
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}
.
In particular, while the trees of order
N
never explicitly appear in expansions, the fact that
\mathbf{X}
is defined on those trees and satisfies an appropriate Hölder bound there is crucial - see the Sewing Lemma - Proposition
2.11
(
[
Gub03
,
Gub06
]
).

Extending
\mathbf{X}
multiplicatively means that
\mathcal{F}
is the natural set of objects for
\mathbf{X}
to act on.
We note that any character path
\mathbf{X}
satisfies
\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},\mathds{1}\rangle=1
.

Definition 2.4
.

A character path satisfies Chen’s relation if, for every
s,u,t\in[0,T]
and
h\in\mathcal{T}
,

\langle(\mathbf{X}_{s,u}\otimes\mathbf{X}_{u,t}),\Delta h\rangle=\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle\;.

Note that above, we overload the notation
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle
for tensor products by setting
\langle u\otimes v,a\otimes b\rangle=\langle u,a\rangle\langle v,b\rangle
and then extending by linearity.
Given a character path
\mathbf{X}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
we define

(2.3)

[\mathbf{X}:f]:=\sup_{0\leq s,t\leq 1\atop s\not=t}\frac{|\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},f\rangle|}{|s-t|^{\alpha|f|}}\;.

The exponent
\alpha
above corresponds to the imposed regularity condition on the driving noise.
Note that one has the obvious bound
[\mathbf{X}:f]\leq\prod_{h\in\mathcal{F}}[\mathbf{X}:h]
.

Definition 2.5
.

A (branched)
\alpha
-rough path is a character path
\mathbf{X}
that satisfies Chen’s relation and has the property that
[\mathbf{X}:h]<\infty
for every
h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}
.

We usually drop the word branched below, just calling
\mathbf{X}
an
\alpha
-rough path.

Proposition 2.6
.

If
\mathbf{X}
is the canonical lift of a smooth function
X
then
\mathbf{X}
is an
\alpha
-rough path for any
\alpha\in(0,1]
.

2.6.
Controlled paths

Definition 2.7
.

We call a map
\mathbf{Y}:[0,T]\rightarrow\Span(\mathcal{T}_{\leq N-1})^{k}
a tree path and a map
\mathbf{Y}:[0,T]\rightarrow\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1})^{k}
a forest path.

We apply operations to tree/forest paths component wise, writing expressions like
\Delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}\in(\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1})\otimes\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}))^{k}
and so on.

We usually use the removal of boldface to indicate the “
\mathds{1}
- component” of a tree path or forest path
\mathbf{Y}
, for instance we write
Y_{s}=\langle\mathds{1},\mathbf{Y}_{s}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and define
\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}
so that

(2.4)

\mathbf{Y}_{s}=Y_{s}\mathds{1}+\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}\;.

Remark 2.8
.

Following the thread from Remark
2.3
, a tree path
\mathbf{Y}
is used as an expansion for the solution
Y
to (
1.3
) while, for each
\mu\in[d]
, a forest path
\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y})
will be used as an expansion of
\sigma_{\mu}(Y)
.

Definition 2.9
.

Fix
I\subset[0,1]
and let
\mathbf{X}
be an
\alpha
-rough path.
Given a forest/tree path
\mathbf{Y}
we define, for any
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
,

[\mathbf{Y}:f]_{I}=\sup_{s,t\in I\atop 0<|s-t|<1}\frac{|R^{\mathbf{Y},f}_{s,t}|}{|t-s|^{(N-|f|)\alpha}}

where the
f
-remainder of
\mathbf{Y}
between
s
and
t
, denoted
R^{\mathbf{Y},f}_{s,t}
, is given by

R^{\mathbf{Y},f}_{s,t}:=\langle f,\mathbf{Y}_{t}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t}\otimes f,\Delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\;.

When
I=[0,1]
we suppress it from that notation, i..e writing
[\mathbf{Y}:f]_{[0,1]}=[\mathbf{Y}:f]
.
We call
\mathbf{Y}
an
\mathbf{X}
-controlled rough forest/tree path if
\max\{[\mathbf{Y}:f]:f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}\}<\infty
.

As in
[
HK15
]
we will define the composition of a function
g\in C^{N-1}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{k})
with a tree path
\mathbf{Y}
via appealing to the Taylor expansion of
g
about
Y_{\bullet}
.
In particular, we define the forest path
g(\mathbf{Y})
by setting

(2.5)

g(\mathbf{Y})_{s}=g(Y_{s})\mathds{1}+P_{\leq N-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}D^{p}g(Y_{s})\big[\big(\underbrace{\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s},\dots,\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\big)\big]\;.

Remark 2.10
.

Note that in (
2.5
) we naturally view
p
-linear forms on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
as
p
-linear forms on
\Span(\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1})^{k}
, for instance

D^{p}g(Y_{s})\big[\big(\underbrace{\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s},\dots,\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\big)\big]=\sum_{h_{1},\dots,h_{p}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}D^{p}g(Y_{s})\big[\big(\langle\mathbf{Y}_{s},h_{1}\rangle,\dots,\langle\mathbf{Y}_{s},h_{p}\rangle\big)\big]\prod_{i=1}^{p}h_{i}\;.

2.7.
Integration of controlled paths

We now explain how we define our rough integral that is needed to define our fixed point problem. Given a forest path
\mathbf{U}
,
\mu\in[d]
, and
s,t\in[0,1]
, we define

\Xi^{\mathbf{U},\mu}_{s,t}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\langle f,\mathbf{U}_{s}\rangle\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},[f]_{\mu}\rangle\;.

The quantity
\Xi_{s,t}
should be seen as a single term in a Riemann -type approximation for the rough integral.
We can now state Gubinelli’s sewing lemma
[
Gub03
,
Gub06
]
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a branched rough path and
\mathbf{U}
be an
\mathbf{X}
-controlled forest path. Then, for any
0\leq s\leq t\leq 1
, we have

(2.6)

\lim_{|\mathcal{P}|\rightarrow 0}\sum_{\{s_{n},s_{n+1}\}\in\mathcal{P}}\Xi^{\mathbf{U},\mu}_{s_{n},s_{n+1}}=:\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{U}_{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}^{(\mu)}_{r}

where the limit above is over partitions
\mathcal{P}
of
[s,t]
with mesh size going to
0
.

We have the estimate

(2.7)

\Bigg|\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{U}_{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}^{(\mu)}_{r}-\Xi^{\mathbf{U},\mu}_{s,t}\Bigg|\leq C(\alpha)|t-s|^{(N+1)\alpha}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}][\mathbf{U}:f]_{[s,t]}\;.

Note that
\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{U}_{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}^{(\mu)}_{r}
is an element of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, not a tree/forest path. Our notion of a solution to (
1.3
) will itself be an expansion so we also want to make sense of the integration of an
\mathbf{X}
-controlled forest path as a tree path rather than just an element
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Therefore we define, given a branched rough path
\mathbf{X}
, an
\mathbf{X}
-controlled tree path
\mathbf{Y}
, and a tuple of functions
\sigma=(\sigma_{\mu}:\mu\in[d])
with
\sigma_{\mu}\in C^{N-1}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, the new tree path

(2.8)

\mathbf{Z}_{\bullet}=\uint_{0}^{\bullet}\sigma(\mathbf{Y})_{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{r}=\sum_{\mu\in[d]}\Big(\int^{\bullet}_{0}\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y})_{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}^{(\mu)}_{r}\Big)\mathds{1}+\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-2}}\langle f,\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y})_{\bullet}\rangle[f]_{\mu}\;.

Note that we typographically distinguish the integral
\int
appearing in (
2.6
) from the integral
\displaystyle\uint
appearing in (
2.8
) since they produce different sorts of objects.

We can now state a precisely what we mean by a “solution” to (
1.3
).

Definition 2.12
.

Given a branched rough path
\mathbf{X}
we say that an
\mathbf{X}
-controlled tree path
\mathbf{Y}
is a solution to (
1.3
) if we have, for
0\leq s\leq 1
,

(2.9)

\mathbf{Y}_{s}=y_{0}\mathds{1}-\Big(\int_{0}^{s}|Y_{r}|^{m-1}Y_{r}\mathrm{d}r\Big)\mathds{1}+\mathbf{Z}_{s}\;.

where
\mathbf{Z}_{s}
is defined as in (
2.8
).

2.8.
Coherence

A necessary (but not sufficient) condition for the tree path
\mathbf{Y}
to be a solution in the sense of Definition
2.12
is that:

(2.10)

\forall[f]_{\mu}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1},\ s\in(0,T],\ \textnormal{ one has }\langle f,\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}_{s})\rangle=\langle[f]_{\mu},\mathbf{Y}_{s}\rangle\;.

The condition (
2.10
) is algebraic and completely local in time.
In order to be able to conclude that
\mathbf{Y}
is a solution in the sense of Definition
2.12
one would additionally have to impose that
\mathbf{Y}
is
\mathbf{X}
-controlled and that, for all
s\in(0,T]
, the
\mathds{1}
coefficient on both sides of (
2.9
) are the same.

Given
Y_{s}
as input, one can use (
2.10
) to calculate either side of (
2.10
) inductively in
|f|
.
This motivates the following definition.

Definition 2.13
.

We define the collection of functions

(\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](\cdot):f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}\;,\;\mu\in[d])\;,\;\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\;,

inductively, as follows.
Given

f=\prod_{i\in I}[f_{i}]_{\mu_{i}}

we set

(2.11)

\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](\cdot)=D^{|I|}\sigma_{\mu}(\cdot)\big[\big(\Upsilon_{\mu_{i}}[f_{i}](\cdot)\big)_{i\in I}\big]\;.

The definition given above is inductive in
|f|
and also fixes the base case
f=\mathds{1}
where we have
\Upsilon_{\mu}[\mathds{1}](\cdot)=\sigma_{\mu}(\cdot)
.

Remark 2.14
.

The functions
\Upsilon_{\mu}[f]
are also known as
elementary differentials
in the analysis of Runge-Kutta methods, see
[
Bro00
]
.

These elementary differentials usually appear with symmetry factors.
We define
S:\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
via recursively setting,
S(\mathds{1})=1
, and

S(f)=\prod_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}}(f_{h}!)S(h)^{f_{h}}\;,

where we recall that
f_{h}\in\mathbb{N}
is the number of instances of
h
in
f
.

A straightforward inductive proof then gives the following lemma.

Lemma 2.15
.

Given a tree path
\mathbf{Y}
, the condition (
2.10
) is equivalent to

(2.12)

\forall[f]_{\mu}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1},\ s\in(0,T],\ \textnormal{ one has }\langle f,\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}_{s})\rangle=\langle[f]_{\mu},\mathbf{Y}_{s}\rangle=\frac{1}{S(f)}\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{s})\;.

Definition 2.16
.

We call a tree path
\mathbf{Y}
that satisfies (
2.10
) (or equivalently (
2.12
))
coherent
.

Remark 2.17
.

We extend
\Upsilon_{\mu}[\bullet]
from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
to
\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
by linearity.
We also define
\Upsilon[\bullet]
on
\Span(\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
by setting, for
h=[f]_{\mu}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
,
\Upsilon[h]=\Upsilon_{\mu}[f]
and then again extend linearly.
We can then write (
2.11
) as

(2.13)

\Upsilon\big[[f]_{\mu}\big](\cdot)=\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](\cdot)=D^{\#f}\sigma_{\mu}(\cdot)\big[\big(\Upsilon[h](\cdot)\big)_{h\in f}\big]\;.

This duplication of notation (giving equivalent definitions of
\Upsilon_{\mu}
on forests and
\Upsilon
on trees) is
convenient because trees appear for the expansion of
Y
while forests appear for the expansion of the right hand side of the equation.

We close this section with the following remark.

Remark 2.18
.

Note that the drift term
-|Y(t)|^{m-1}Y(t)dt
didn’t play much of a role in this section, in particular it has no direct effect on the coherence coefficients
\Upsilon_{\mu}[\cdot](\cdot)
.
However, since
\big|\int_{s}^{t}|Y(r)|^{m-1}Y(r)dr\big|\lesssim|s-t|
, this term is negligible compared to the other terms in our local expansion for
Y
.
In particular, by virtue of (
2.9
), we have

(2.14)

R^{\mathbf{Z},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}=Z_{t}-Z_{s}-\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},\delta\mathbf{Z}_{s}\rangle=R_{s,t}^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}+\int_{s}^{t}|Y(r)|^{m-1}Y(r)dr\;,

where
\delta\mathbf{Z}
is defined via the same convention as in (
2.4
) and we used that, by coherence,

\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},\delta\mathbf{Z}_{s}\rangle=\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle\langle h,\mathbf{Z}_{s}\rangle\;=\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle\langle h,\mathbf{Y}_{s}\rangle\;.

The role of the drift becomes important only in the final step of our argument in Section
4
.

3.
Grafting, coherence, and remainder estimates

3.1.
Grafting operations

We introduce grafting operations which will later be shown to be “adjoints” of the co-product under a particular inner product we introduce later.

In words, given
\tilde{f},f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
, the grafting of
\tilde{f}
onto
f
sums over all the ways of attaching each of the trees in
\tilde{h}\in\tilde{f}
to a vertex of
f
with an edge, or concatenating
\tilde{h}
to the forest
f
.
More precisely, given

(3.1)

\tilde{f}=\prod_{j\in J}\tilde{h}_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}\textnormal{ and }f=\prod_{i\in I}[f_{i}]_{\mu_{i}}\;.

we inductively define

(3.2)

\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f=\sum_{K\subset J}\Big(\prod_{j\in J\setminus K}\tilde{h}_{j}\Big)\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}\Big(\prod_{i\in I}\Big[\Big(\prod_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{k}\Big)\curvearrowright f_{i}\Big]_{\mu_{i}}\Big)\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}\;.

The definition above is inductive in
|f|
, with the base case corresponding to
f=\mathds{1}
for which we have
I=\emptyset
in (
3.1
) and so one must choose
K=\emptyset
in the first sum of (
3.2
) – in particular we have
\tilde{f}\curvearrowright\mathds{1}=\tilde{f}
.
Additionally, we note that
\langle\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f,\bar{f}\rangle\not=0\Rightarrow|\tilde{f}|+|f|=|\bar{f}|
.
We give an example grafting computation below:
