\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

Remark 3.1
.

The operation
\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f
is a decorated version of the “grafting forests over forests” operation of
[
Foi13
, Section 1.5]
, this is essentially a version of the Grossman-Larson product
[
GL89
]
.

3.2.
Inner products and the adjoint relation

Definition 3.2
.

We define a new inner product on
\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
by inductively setting

•

\bm{\langle\langle}\mathds{1},\mathds{1}\bm{\rangle\rangle}=1

•

\bm{\langle\langle}[f]_{\mu},[g]_{\mu^{\prime}}\bm{\rangle\rangle}=1\{\mu=\mu^{\prime}\}\bm{\langle\langle}f,g\bm{\rangle\rangle}

•

For
h_{i},g_{j}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
,

\bm{\langle\langle}\prod_{i\in I}h_{i},\prod_{j\in J}g_{j}\bm{\rangle\rangle}=\sum_{\theta:I\rightarrow J\atop\mathrm{biject.}}\prod_{i\in I}\bm{\langle\langle}h_{i},g_{\theta(i)}\bm{\rangle\rangle}\;,

We extend this inner product to
\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\otimes\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
by setting, for
f,g,\bar{f},\bar{g}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
,

\bm{\langle\langle}f\otimes g,\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}\bm{\rangle\rangle}=\bm{\langle\langle}f,\bar{f}\bm{\rangle\rangle}\bm{\langle\langle}g,\bar{g}\bm{\rangle\rangle}\;.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that, for
f,g\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
,

(3.3)

\bm{\langle\langle}f,g\bm{\rangle\rangle}=1\{f=g\}S(f)=S(f)\langle f,g\rangle\;.

In particular, (
2.12
) can be rewritten as :

\forall[f]_{\mu}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1},\ s\in(0,T],\ \textnormal{ one has }\bm{\langle\langle}f,\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}_{s})\bm{\rangle\rangle}=\bm{\langle\langle}[f]_{\mu},\mathbf{Y}_{s}\bm{\rangle\rangle}=\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{s})\;.

The following lemma is straightforward to prove from our definition of
\bm{\langle\langle}\bullet,\bullet\bm{\rangle\rangle}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Given any
\tilde{h}_{j},h_{i}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}
and also
a_{n}\in\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})\otimes\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}})
one has

\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\big(\prod_{j\in J}\tilde{h}_{j}\big)\otimes\big(\prod_{i\in I}h_{i}\big),\prod_{n\in N}a_{n}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{J}:J\rightarrow N\\
\theta_{I}:I\rightarrow N\end{subarray}}\prod_{n\in N}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\big(\prod_{j\in\theta_{J}^{-1}(n)}\tilde{h}_{j}\big)\otimes\big(\prod_{i\in\theta_{I}^{-1}(n)}h_{i}\big),a_{n}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}

Our main purpose for introducing the inner products
\bm{\langle\langle}\bullet,\bullet\bm{\rangle\rangle}
is that it is convenient for stating and proving following theorem.

Theorem 3.5
.

For any
\tilde{f},f,g\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
, we have

(3.4)

\bm{\langle\langle}\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f,g\bm{\rangle\rangle}=\bm{\langle\langle}\tilde{f}\otimes f,\Delta g\bm{\rangle\rangle}\;.

Proof.

We give an inductive proof with the induction being in
|f|
. For the base case we have
f=\mathds{1}
in which case (
3.4
) is immediate.
We now prove the inductive step.
We write
g=\prod_{n\in N}[g_{n}]_{\bar{\mu}_{n}}
and also write
\tilde{f}
and
f
as in (
3.1
). We then have

\begin{split}\bm{\langle\langle}\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f,g\bm{\rangle\rangle}=&\sum_{K\subset J}\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big(\prod_{j\in J\setminus K}\tilde{h}_{j}\Big)\prod_{i\in I}\Big[\Big(\prod_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{k}\Big)\curvearrowright f_{i}\Big]_{\mu_{i}},\prod_{n\in N}[g_{n}]_{\bar{\mu}_{n}}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\\
=&\sum_{K\subset J}\sum_{\sigma:(J\setminus K)\sqcup I\rightarrow N\atop\textnormal{biject.}}\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}\Big(\prod_{j\in J\setminus K}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\tilde{h}_{j},[g_{\sigma(j)}]_{\bar{\mu}_{\sigma(j)}}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\\
{}&\quad\times\Big(\prod_{i\in I}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big[\Big(\prod_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{k}\Big)\curvearrowright f_{i}\Big]_{\mu_{i}},[g_{\sigma(i)}]_{\bar{\mu}_{\sigma(i)}}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\\
=&\sum_{K\subset J}\sum_{\sigma:(J\setminus K)\sqcup I\rightarrow N\atop\textnormal{biject.}}\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}\Big(\prod_{j\in J\setminus K}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\tilde{h}_{j},[g_{\sigma(j)}]_{\bar{\mu}_{\sigma(j)}}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\\
{}&\quad\times\Big(\prod_{i\in I}1\{\mu_{i}=\bar{\mu}_{\sigma(j)}\}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big(\prod_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{k}\Big)\otimes f_{i},\Delta g_{\sigma(i)}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\;.\end{split}

Above, in the second equality we used the third identity of Definition
3.2
, and in the third equality we used our the third identity of Definition
3.2
followed by our induction hypothesis.

One the other hand,
\Delta g=\sum_{M\subset N}\big(\prod_{m\in M}[g_{m}]_{\bar{\mu}_{m}}\otimes\mathds{1}\Big)\Big(\prod_{n\in N\setminus M}(\mathrm{id}\otimes[\bullet]_{\bar{\mu}_{n}})\Delta g_{n}\Big)

which gives

\begin{split}{}&\bm{\langle\langle}\tilde{f}\otimes f,\Delta g\bm{\rangle\rangle}\\
=&\sum_{M\subset N}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\tilde{f}\otimes f,\Big(\prod_{m\in M}[g_{m}]_{\bar{\mu}_{m}}\otimes\mathds{1}\Big)\Big(\prod_{n\in N\setminus M}(\mathrm{id}\otimes[\cdot]_{\bar{\mu}_{n}})\Delta g_{n}\Big)\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\\
=&\sum_{M\subset N}\sum_{\theta_{J}:J\rightarrow N}\sum_{\theta_{I}:I\rightarrow N}\Big(\prod_{m\in M}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big(\prod_{j\in\theta_{J}^{-1}(m)}\tilde{h}_{j}\Big)\otimes\Big(\prod_{i\in\theta_{I}^{-1}(m)}[f_{i}]_{\mu_{i}}\Big),[g_{m}]_{\bar{\mu}_{m}}\otimes\mathds{1}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\\
{}&\quad\times\Big(\prod_{n\in N\setminus M}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big(\prod_{j\in\theta_{J}^{-1}(n)}\tilde{h}_{j}\Big)\otimes\Big(\prod_{i\in\theta_{I}^{-1}(n)}[f_{i}]_{\mu_{i}}\Big),(\mathrm{id}\otimes[\cdot]_{\bar{\mu}_{n}})\Delta g_{n}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\\
=&\sum_{M\subset N}\sum_{\theta_{J}:J\rightarrow N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{I}:I\rightarrow N\setminus M\\
\textnormal{biject.}\end{subarray}}\Big(\prod_{m\in M}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big(\prod_{j\in\theta_{J}^{-1}(m)}\tilde{h}_{j}\Big),[g_{m}]_{\bar{\mu}_{m}}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\\
{}&\quad\times\Big(\prod_{i\in I}1\{\mu_{i}=\bar{\mu}_{\theta_{I}(i)}\}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big(\prod_{j\in\theta_{J}^{-1}(\theta_{I}(i))}\tilde{h}_{j}\Big)\otimes f_{i},\Delta g_{\theta_{I}(i)}\big\rangle\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\\
=&\sum_{M\subset N}\sum_{K\subset J}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi:J\setminus K\rightarrow M\\
\textnormal{biject.}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{I}:I\rightarrow N\setminus M\\
\textnormal{biject.}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}\Big(\prod_{j\in J\setminus K}\big\langle\tilde{h}_{j},[g_{\pi(m)}]_{\bar{\mu}_{\pi(m)}}\big\rangle\Big)\\
{}&\quad\times\Big(\prod_{i\in I}1\{\mu_{i}=\bar{\mu}_{\theta_{I}(i)}\}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big(\prod_{j\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{j}\Big)\otimes f_{i},\Delta g_{\theta_{I}(i)}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\\
=&\sum_{K\subset J}\sum_{\sigma:(J\setminus K)\sqcup I\rightarrow N\atop\textnormal{biject.}}\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}\Big(\prod_{j\in J\setminus K}\bm{\langle\langle}\tilde{h}_{j},[g_{\sigma(j)}]_{\bar{\mu}_{\sigma(j)}}\bm{\rangle\rangle}\Big)\\
{}&\quad\times\Big(\prod_{i\in I}1\{\mu_{i}=\bar{\mu}_{\sigma(j)}\}\bm{\Big\langle\Big\langle}\Big(\prod_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{k}\Big)\otimes f_{i},\Delta g_{\sigma(i)}\bm{\Big\rangle\Big\rangle}\Big)\;.\end{split}

In the second equality above we used Lemma
3.4
. In the third equality we above we used the fact that the line before vanishes unless
\theta_{I}
is a bijection from
I
to
N\setminus M
.

In the fourth equality we used that the line before vanishes unless
|\theta_{J}^{-1}(m)|=1
for every
m\in M
.
Therefore the sum over the map
\theta_{J}
in the line before can be written as a sum over
K=\theta_{J}^{-1}(N\setminus M)
,
\pi=\theta_{J}\restriction_{J\setminus K}
and
\theta=(\theta_{J}\restriction_{K})\circ\theta_{I}^{-1}
.
In the last equality we exchange the sums over
\pi
and
\theta_{I}
, and
M
for just a single sum over
\sigma
.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The result of Theorem
3.5
was actually first obtained in
[
Hof03
, Proposition 4.4]
, and reflects the duality between the Grossman-Larson and Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebras.

3.3.
Coherence and grafting

The next lemma states a generalisation of (
2.13
) and shows that maps
\Upsilon_{\mu}
interact well with our grafting procedure.

Lemma 3.7
.

Given any
\tilde{f},f\in\mathcal{F}
with
|\tilde{f}|+|f|\leq N-1
, and
\mu\in[d]
one has

\Upsilon_{\mu}[\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f]=D^{\#\tilde{f}}\Upsilon_{\mu}[f]\big[\big(\Upsilon[\tilde{h}]\big)_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{f}}\big]\;.

Proof.

We prove the general case by induction in
|f|
- the base case when
f=\mathds{1}
is immediate.

We write
f
and
\tilde{f}
as in (
3.1
).
Now we note that, by Leibnitz rule and the definition of
\Upsilon
, for any
v=(v_{j})_{j\in J}\in(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{J}
,

(3.5)

\begin{split}{}&D^{\#\tilde{f}}\Upsilon_{\mu}[f][v]\\
{}&\quad=\sum_{(J_{i}:i\in I)}D^{\#f+\#\tilde{f}-\sum_{i\in I}|J_{i}|}\sigma_{\mu}\Big[\big(D^{|J_{i}|}\Upsilon_{\mu_{i}}[f_{i}][v_{J_{i}}]\big)_{i\in I}\sqcup v_{J^{c}}\Big]\;,\end{split}

where above the sum is over collections of disjoint subsets
(J_{i}:i\in I)
,
J_{i}\subset J
and we write
v_{J_{i}}=(v_{j})_{j\in J_{i}}
and
J^{c}=J\setminus\big(\bigcup_{i\in I}J_{i}\big)
.

On the other hand, using the definition of the grafting procedure followed by that of
\Upsilon_{\mu}

\begin{split}{}&\Upsilon_{\mu}[\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f]\\
=&\sum_{K\subset J}\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}\Upsilon_{\mu}\Big[\Big(\prod_{j\in J\setminus K}\tilde{h}_{j}\Big)\Big(\prod_{i\in I}\Big[\big(\prod_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{k}\big)\curvearrowright f_{i}\Big]_{\mu_{i}}\Big)\Big]\\
=&\sum_{K\subset J}\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}D^{|J\setminus K|+\#f}\sigma_{\mu}\Bigg[\Big(\Upsilon[\tilde{h}_{j}]\Big)_{j\in J\setminus K}\sqcup\Big(\Upsilon_{\mu_{i}}\Big[\big(\prod_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{k}\big)\curvearrowright f_{i}\Big]\Big)_{i\in I}\Bigg]\;.\end{split}

Now, by our induction hypothesis, for any
\theta:K\rightarrow I
and
i\in I
we have

\Upsilon_{\mu_{i}}\Big[\big(\prod_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\tilde{h}_{k}\big)\curvearrowright f_{i}\Big]=D^{|\theta^{-1}(i)|}\Upsilon_{\mu_{i}}[f_{i}]\Big[\big(\Upsilon[\tilde{h}_{k}]\big)_{k\in\theta^{-1}(i)}\Big]\;.

Inserting the above calculation into the previous equation gives

\begin{split}{}&\Upsilon_{\mu}[\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f]\\
{}&=\sum_{K\subset J}\sum_{\theta:K\rightarrow I}D^{|J\setminus K|+\#f}\sigma_{\mu}\Big[\big(D^{|\theta^{-1}(i)|}\Upsilon_{\mu_{i}}[f_{i}][(\Upsilon[\tilde{h}_{j}])_{j\in\theta^{-1}(i)}]\big)_{i\in I}\sqcup(\Upsilon[\tilde{h}_{j}])_{j\in J\setminus K}\Big]\;,\end{split}

which finishes the proof by comparing with (
3.5
), identifying
J_{i}\leftrightarrow\theta^{-1}(i)
, and setting
v=(\Upsilon[\tilde{h}_{j}])_{j\in J}
.
∎

In what follows we define, for
g\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
and
\mu\in[d]
,
\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[g](\cdot)=\frac{1}{S(g)}\Upsilon_{\mu}[g](\cdot)
and, for
h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}
,
\bm{\Upsilon}[h](\cdot)=\frac{1}{S(h)}\Upsilon[h](\cdot)
.
We also write

\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}(\cdot)=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[g](\cdot)g\;.

In particular,
\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\Span(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1})^{k}

and for any
g\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
we have
\bm{\langle\langle}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu},g\bm{\rangle\rangle}=\Upsilon_{\mu}[g]
.
We also define

\bm{\Upsilon}(\cdot)=\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\bm{\Upsilon}[h](\cdot)h\;.

We see that if a tree path
\mathbf{Y}
is coherent then, for any
s\in(0,1]
, we have
\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}=\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s})
and, for every
\mu\in[d]
,
\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}_{s})=\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}(Y_{s})
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Given any
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
and
\mu\in[d]
,

\sum_{\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\langle\tilde{f}\otimes f,\Delta\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}\rangle\tilde{f}=P_{\leq N-|f|-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f]\Big[\underbrace{(\bm{\Upsilon},\dots,\bm{\Upsilon})}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\Big]\;.

Proof.

We observe that

\begin{split}\langle\tilde{f}\otimes f,\Delta\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}\rangle\tilde{f}=&\frac{1}{S(\tilde{f})S(f)}\bm{\langle\langle}\tilde{f}\otimes f,\Delta\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}\bm{\rangle\rangle}\tilde{f}\\
=&\frac{1}{S(\tilde{f})S(f)}\bm{\langle\langle}\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f,\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}\bm{\rangle\rangle}\tilde{f}=\frac{1\{|f|+|\tilde{f}|<N\}}{S(\tilde{f})S(f)}\Upsilon_{\mu}[\tilde{f}\curvearrowright f]\tilde{f}\\
=&\frac{1\{|f|+|\tilde{f}|<N\}}{S(\tilde{f})S(f)}D^{\#\tilde{f}}\Upsilon_{\mu}[f]\Big[\big(\Upsilon[\tilde{h}]\big)_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{f}}\Big]\tilde{f}\\
=&1\{|f|+|\tilde{f}|<N\}\frac{\mathrm{Multi}(f)}{\#\tilde{f}!}D^{\#\tilde{f}}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f]\Big[\big(\bm{\Upsilon}[\tilde{h}]\tilde{h}\big)_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{f}}\Big]\;.\end{split}

In the first equality above we are using (
3.3
) and in the second equality we are applying Theorem
3.5
.
In the third equality above we are using Lemma
3.7
, and for the third equality we define the multinomial coefficient

\mathrm{Multi}(\tilde{f})=\frac{\#\tilde{f}!}{\prod_{\tilde{h}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\tilde{f}_{\tilde{h}}!}

where
\tilde{f}_{\tilde{h}}
in again the number of instances of
\tilde{h}
in
\tilde{f}
, so that we have

S(\tilde{f})=\frac{\#\tilde{f}!}{\mathrm{Multi}(\tilde{f})}\prod_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{f}}S(\tilde{h})\;.

It follows that

(3.6)

\begin{split}\sum_{\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\langle\tilde{f}\otimes f,\Delta\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}\rangle\tilde{f}=&\sum_{\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}\atop|\tilde{f}|<N-|f|}\frac{\mathrm{Multi}(\tilde{f})}{\#\tilde{f}!}D^{\#\tilde{f}}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f]\Big[\big(\bm{\Upsilon}[\tilde{h}]\tilde{h}\big)_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{f}}\Big]\\
=&\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}\atop|\tilde{f}|<N-|f|}1\{\#f=p\}\mathrm{Multi}(\tilde{f})D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f]\Big[\big(\bm{\Upsilon}[\tilde{h}]\big)_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{f}}\Big]\\
=&\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{h_{1},\dots,h_{p}\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f]\Big[\big(\bm{\Upsilon}[h_{i}]h_{i}\big)_{i=1}^{p}\Big]\\
=&P_{\leq N-|f|-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f]\Big[\underbrace{(\bm{\Upsilon},\dots,\bm{\Upsilon})}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\Big]\;.\end{split}

∎

The above lemma gives us the following corollary, the main result of this section.
It expresses remainders at higher levels in terms of remainders at level 0.

Corollary 3.9
.

For any coherent tree path
\mathbf{Y}
,
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
, and
\mu\in[d]
, we have

(3.7)

\begin{split}R^{\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}),f}_{s,t}&=\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{t})-\Big\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},P_{\leq N-|f|-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{s})\Big[\underbrace{(\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s}),\dots,\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s}))}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\Big]\Big\rangle\;.\end{split}

Proof.

Using the definition of coherence we can write

\begin{split}R^{\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}),f}_{s,t}=&\langle f,\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})\rangle-\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t}\otimes f,\Delta\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})\rangle\\
=&\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{t})-\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t}\otimes f,\Delta\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}(Y_{s})\rangle.\end{split}

We then use Lemma
3.8
to see that

\begin{split}\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t}\otimes f,\Delta\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}(Y_{s})\rangle=&\Big\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},\sum_{\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\langle\tilde{f}\otimes f,\Delta\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}(Y_{s})\rangle\tilde{f}\Big\rangle\\
=&\Big\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},P_{\leq N-|f|-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{s})\Big[\underbrace{(\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s}),\dots,\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s}))}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\Big]\Big\rangle\;.\end{split}

∎

Remark 3.10
.

Note that if we know that the functions
(\sigma_{\mu})_{\mu=1}^{d}
are actually smooth then the
\Upsilon_{\mu}
make sense on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{\tiny all}}
and it easy to check that the various lemmas and corollaries of this subsection hold without any constraint on the orders of the participating forests.

3.4.
The main remainder estimate

In order to estimate (
3.7
) we will use the Taylor expansion/remainder formula of
[
Hai14
, Proposition A.1]
.

We will want to impose a stopping rule on generating our Taylor expansion that takes into the consideration the order forests that are produced, a Taylor expansion like
[
Hai14
, Proposition A.1]
is convenient because it lets us enforce a rule on how the expansion is iteratively generated.

We say
A\subset\mathcal{F}
is
full
if it is non-empty, finite, and, for any
f\in A
, we have

\{\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}:\tilde{f}_{h}\leq f_{h}\textnormal{ for every }h\in\mathcal{F}\}\subset A\;.

Note that, for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\leq M}
is full.

We now fix some arbitrary total order on
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}
.
For any
A\subset\mathcal{F}
we define a “boundary” of
A
by setting

(3.8)

\partial A=\left\{f\in\mathcal{F}\setminus(A\cup\mathds{1}):(f_{h}-1\{h=\mathfrak{m}(f)\}:h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1})\in A\right\}\;,

where

\mathfrak{m}(f)=\min\{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}:f_{h}\not=0\}\;

is the minimal tree appearing in the non-empty forest
f
.
In words,
f\in\partial A
if
f
is non-empty, does not belong to
A
, but if one reduces the number of instances of the minimal tree appearing in
f
by
1
then the resulting forest does belong to
A
.

We can then state the formulation of
[
Hai14
, Proposition A.1]
we will use.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{F}
be full and
F\in C^{K+1}([0,1]^{\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}})
where
K=\max_{f\in A}\#f
, then

F(\underline{1})=\sum_{f\in A}\frac{\partial^{f}F(0)}{f!}+\sum_{f\in\partial A}\int_{[0,1]^{\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}}\partial^{f}F(y)Q^{f}(dy)

where
\underline{1}=(1:h\in\mathcal{F})\in[0,1]^{\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}
is vector of all ones and we are using standard notation for derivatives and monomials indexed by multi-indices
f=(f_{h}:h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1})\in\mathcal{F}
, and for each
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
Q^{f}(dy)
is measure of total mass
1
on
[0,1]^{\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}
.

We can now state the key lemma for this section.

Corollary 3.12
.

Suppose the tree path
\mathbf{Y}
is coherent, then, for any
\mu\in[d]
and
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
,

(3.9)

\begin{split}&|R^{\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}),f}_{s,t}|\lesssim\sup\left\{\big|D\Upsilon_{\mu}[f]\big(Y_{s}+a\big)\big|\cdot|R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}|:a\in\mathbb{R}^{k},|a|\leq E_{s,t}^{\mathbf{Y}}+|Y_{t}-Y_{s}|\right\}\\
{}&+\sup\left\{\Big|D^{\#\tilde{f}}\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{s}+z)\prod_{h\in\tilde{f}}\Upsilon[h](Y_{s})\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle\Big|:\ \begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|-1}\\
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k},|z|\leq E_{s,t}^{\mathbf{Y}}\end{array}\right\}\;,\end{split}

where

(3.10)

E_{s,t}^{\mathbf{Y}}=\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}|\Upsilon[h](Y_{s})\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle|\;.

Before turning to the proof, we point out that (
3.9
) estimates
R^{\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}),f}_{s,t}
by treating it as a multivariable Taylor remainder - this is why we see suprema of derivatives on the right hand side above.
Being careful about the constraints on the arguments of these derivatives will be important, in particular when working with coefficients
\sigma
that are allowed to grow at infinity, see Assumption
1.2
.

Proof.

Given any
x=(x_{h})\in[0,1]^{\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}
we define

\mathbf{Y}_{s,t}(x)=\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}x_{h}\bm{\Upsilon}[h](Y_{s})\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle\;.

We then have

Y_{s}+\mathbf{Y}_{s,t}(\underline{1})+R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}=Y_{t}

We now define the function
F:[0,1]^{\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
given by

F(x)=\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f]\Big(Y_{s}+\mathbf{Y}_{s,t}(x)\Big).

Now, by Corollary
3.9
and adding and subtracting
F(\underline{1})
, we have

(3.11)

\begin{split}R^{\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}),f}_{s,t}&\leq\Big|\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{s}+\mathbf{Y}_{s,t}(\underline{1})+R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t})-\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{s}+\mathbf{Y}_{s,t}(\underline{1}))\Big|\\
&\quad+\Big|F(\underline{1})-\Big\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},P_{\leq N-|f|-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f]\Big[\underbrace{(\bm{\Upsilon},\dots,\bm{\Upsilon})}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\Big]\Big\rangle\Big|\;.\end{split}

By the mean value theorem the first term on the right hand side of (
3.11
) is bounded by

\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\big|D\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{s}+\mathbf{Y}_{s,t}(\underline{1})+\lambda R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t})\big|\cdot|R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}|\;,

which in turn is bounded by the first term on the right hand side of (
3.9
).

We now claim that

(3.12)

\begin{split}\sum_{\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|=1}}\frac{\partial^{\tilde{f}}F(0)}{\tilde{f}!}=&\Big\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},P_{\leq N-|f|-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{s})\Big[\underbrace{(\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s}),\dots,\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s}))}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\Big]\Big\rangle\;\;.\end{split}

To verify that (
3.12
) holds, note that we can substitute into (
3.12
) the right hand side of the first line of (
3.6
) and also use that

\frac{\mathrm{Multi}(\tilde{f})}{\#\tilde{f}!}=\frac{1}{\tilde{f}!}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Big\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},D^{\#\tilde{f}}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{s})\Big[\big(\bm{\Upsilon}[\tilde{h}](Y_{s})\tilde{h}\big)_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{f}}\Big]\Big\rangle=\partial^{\tilde{f}}F(0)\;.

Applying Proposition
3.11
to
F(\underline{1})
with
A=\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1-|f|}
then gives us

(3.13)

\begin{split}{}&F(\underline{1})-\Big\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},P_{\leq N-|f|-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p!}D^{p}\bm{\Upsilon}_{\mu}[f](Y_{s})\Big[\underbrace{(\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s}),\dots,\bm{\Upsilon}(Y_{s}))}_{p\textnormal{-times}}\Big]\Big\rangle\\
{}&=\sum_{\tilde{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1-|f|}}\int_{[0,1]^{\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}}(\partial^{\tilde{f}}F)(y)Q^{\tilde{f}}(dy)\;.\end{split}

We then see the second term on the right hand side of (
3.11
) is bounded by the second term on the right hand side of (
3.9
).
∎

4.
Proof of main Theorems

Before proceeding to the details of the proof we give a brief overview. The key idea is contained in the
interior regularity estimate
, Lemma
4.2
,
which in turn builds on the Sewing Lemma, Proposition
2.11
, and Corollary
3.12
. More precisely, the Sewing Lemma provides an estimate of order

(N+1)\alpha
on the rough integral
Z
in terms of the various order bounds
[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]
and the semi-norms
[\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}):f]
which measure the quality of local approximations of
all coefficients.
According to Corollary
3.12
the semi-norms
[\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}):f]
can be controlled in terms of

•

the size of the coefficients
\Upsilon
on the relevant interval,

•

the order bounds
[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]
, as well as

•

the remainder
R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}
.

It is important to note the relevant estimate (
3.9
) is
linear
in
|R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}|
and that
|R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}|
describes an error of order
N\alpha
in the local description of
Y
. Furthermore, the quantity
|R^{\mathbf{Y},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}|

can be easily estimated in terms of the corresponding quantity for
Z
. Combining all of these estimates one obtains an estimate on the error of the local approximation of order
N+1
of
Z
in terms of (the coefficients
\Upsilon
, the order bounds
[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]
and) the quantities
|R^{\mathbf{Z},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}|
which measure the error in the approximation of order
N\alpha
. This can be seen as a regularity improvement from
N\alpha
to
(N+1)\alpha
, which in turn allows to absorb the term
|R^{\mathbf{Z},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}|
provided one works on a small-enough interval.
The proof of Theorem
1.4
then mostly consists of plugging (a truncated version of) the
interior regularity estimate
, Lemma
4.2
, into Lemma
1.11
, which captures the damping of the non-linear term as discussed in the introduction, and choosing appropriate parameters depending on the growth assumption on the coefficient
\sigma
. The final iteration argument (see Step 3 of the Proof of Theorem
1.4
1)) is an adaptation of the
additive noise case
,
[
MW20a
, Proof of Lemma 2.1, Step 6]
.

We start the argument with the following simple lemma that translates Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
into estimates on
\Upsilon[h]
for
h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}
and
0\leq p\leq N-|h|+1
.

Suppose that Assumption
1.1
holds, then

(4.1)

\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}|D^{p}\Upsilon[h](y)|\lesssim C_{\sigma}^{|h|}\;.

Suppose instead that Assumption
1.2
holds, then

(4.2)

|D^{p}\Upsilon[h](y)|\lesssim C_{\sigma}^{|h|}\langle y\rangle^{(\gamma-1)|h|+1-p}\;.

Proof.

We treat the case
p=0
, the case of
p\not=0
can be treated similarly - the constraint on
p
comes from the fact that we control only
N
derivatives of the
(\sigma_{\mu})_{\mu\in d}
.

A straightforward inductive argument shows that

|\Upsilon[h](y)|\lesssim\prod_{u\in N_{h}}|D^{c(u)}\sigma_{\mu(u)}(y)|\;,

where on the right hand side we are taking an appropriate operator norm,
N_{h}
denotes the set of nodes of the tree
h
and, given
u\in N_{h}
, we write
c(u)
for the number of children nodes of
u
in
h
(that is, the number of nodes directly attached to
u
that are further from the root) and
\mu(u)\in[d]
for the value of the node decoration that
u
carries.

Using the fact that the cardinality of
N_{h}
is
|h|
, the bound in the case of Assumption
1.1
follows immediately.

In the case of Assumption
1.2
, the first bound follows from the fact that
\sum_{u\in N_{h}}c(u)
is just the number of edges of
h
which is
|h|-1
.
∎

We can alternatively formulate the above lemma by saying that, for any
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
and
\mu\in[d]
, we have, uniformly over
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
0\leq p\leq N
,

|D^{p}\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](y)|\lesssim\begin{cases}C_{\sigma}^{|f|+1}&\text{ under the Assumption\penalty\ \ref{Ass-Sigma-Bounded},}\\
C_{\sigma}^{|f|+1}\langle y\rangle^{(\gamma-1)(|f|+1)+1-p}&\text{ under Assumption\penalty\ \ref{Ass-Sigma-Polynomial}.}\end{cases}

We are now ready to pass to the proofs of Theorems
1.4
and Theorems
1.5
.
The following “interior regularity estimate” which gives a control of the
C^{\alpha}
norm of
Z
and
Y
in terms of its lower regularity
L^{\infty}
norm is
key to both arguments.
To keep the length of some formulas within reason we use the following shorthand notation combining some of the quantities appearing on the right hand side of (
3.9
)

(4.3)

\begin{split}U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)&:=\sup\big\{\big|D\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{s}+a)\big|:s,t\in I,|a|\leq E_{s,t}^{\mathbf{Y}}+|Y_{t}-Y_{s}|\big\}\;,\\
U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},I)&:=\sup\bigg\{\big|D^{\#\bar{f}}\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{s}+z)\prod_{h\in\bar{f}}\Upsilon[h](Y_{s})\big|:\begin{array}[]{c}s,t\in I,\\
|z|\leq E_{s,t}^{\mathbf{Y}}\end{array}\bigg\}\;.\end{split}

Below, for any
j
, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{=j}
the set
\{f\in\mathcal{F}:|f|=j\}
.

Lemma 4.2

(Interior regularity)
.

Let
\mathbf{Y}
solve (
1.3
) on
[0,1]
in the sense of Definition
2.12
and let
\mathbf{Z}
be given by (
2.8
). Let
I\subseteq[0,1]
be a closed interval of length
L

and assume that
L
is small enough to ensure that for all forests
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
and all
\mu\in[d]

(4.4)

\displaystyle L^{(|f|+1)\alpha}\left[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}\right]U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)\leq\varepsilon\;,

for a suitably small
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\alpha,d,k)\leq 1
.
Then we have the estimates

\displaystyle L^{N\alpha}[\mathbf{Z}\colon\mathds{1}]_{I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\Bigg\{L^{(|f|+1)\alpha+1}U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)\|Y\|_{I}^{m}+\max_{\bar{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|-1}}L^{(|\bar{f}|+|f|+1)\alpha}U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},I)[\mathbf{X}:\bar{f}]\Bigg\}

(4.5)

\displaystyle+\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{=N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}L^{N\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]\|\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{\bullet})\|_{I}\,.

Proof.

Part 1: Application of Sewing Lemma.

For
0\leq s\leq t\leq 1
, the estimate (
2.7
) implies that

\Big|Z_{t}-Z_{s}-\Xi^{\sigma(\mathbf{Y})}_{s,t}\Big|\lesssim|t-s|^{(N+1)\alpha}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}\left[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}\right]\left[\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}):f\right]_{[s,t]}\;,

where

\Xi^{\sigma(\mathbf{Y})}_{s,t}:=\sum_{\mu\in[d]}\Xi^{\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y})}_{s,t}=\sum_{\mu\in[d]}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},[f]_{\mu}\rangle\langle f,\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y})_{s}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\;.

By coherence of
\mathbf{Y}
, i.e. by (
2.10
), we get,

\displaystyle\Xi^{\sigma(\mathbf{Y})}_{s,t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle\langle h,\mathbf{Y}_{s}\rangle+\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{=N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},[f]_{\mu}\rangle\langle f,\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y})_{s}\rangle\;.

Recalling that
\delta\mathbf{Y}=\delta\mathbf{Z}
this leads to

\displaystyle|R^{\mathbf{Z},\mathds{1}}_{s,t}|

\displaystyle=\Big|Z_{t}-Z_{s}-\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|Z_{t}-Z_{s}-\Xi^{\sigma(\mathbf{Y})}_{s,t}\Big|+\Big|\Xi^{\sigma(\mathbf{Y})}_{s,t}-\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},\delta\mathbf{Y}_{s}\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim|t-s|^{(N+1)\alpha}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}\left[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}\right]\big[\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}):f\big]_{[s,t]}

\displaystyle+|t-s|^{N\alpha}\sum_{\mathcal{F}_{=N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]\cdot|\langle f,\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y})_{s}\rangle|\;.

Dividing by
|t-s|^{N\alpha}
and taking the supremum over all
s<t
in
I
we arrive at

\displaystyle[\mathbf{Z}\colon\mathds{1}]_{I}

\displaystyle\lesssim L^{\alpha}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}\left[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}\right]\big[\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}):f\big]_{I}

(4.6)

\displaystyle+\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{=N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]\cdot\|\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{\bullet})\|_{I}\;.

Part 2: Application of Corollary
3.12
.

We use the estimate (
3.9
) in Corollary
3.12
in the form

(4.7)

[\sigma_{\mu}(\mathbf{Y}):f]_{I}\lesssim L^{|f|\alpha}U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)[\mathbf{Y}:\mathds{1}]_{I}+\max_{\bar{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|-1}}L^{(|\bar{f}|+|f|-N)\alpha}U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},I)[\mathbf{X}:\bar{f}]\;.

Note that
|\bar{f}|\geq N-|f|
, so that the exponent
(|\bar{f}|+|f|-N)\alpha
in the second term on the RHS of (
4.7
) is always non-negative.

We plug this estimate into the RHS of (
4.6
) resulting in

\displaystyle[\mathbf{Z}\colon\mathds{1}]_{I}

\displaystyle\lesssim L^{\alpha}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Bigg\{L^{|f|\alpha}U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)[\mathbf{Y}:\mathds{1}]_{I}+\max_{\bar{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|-1}}L^{(|\bar{f}|+|f|-N)\alpha}U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},I)[\mathbf{X}:\bar{f}]\Bigg\}

(4.8)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathcal{F}_{=N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]\cdot\|\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{\bullet})\|_{I}\;.

The key observation at this point is that the terms
[\mathbf{Y}:\mathds{1}]_{I}
on the RHS can be replaced by
[\mathbf{Z}\colon\mathds{1}]_{I}
. Indeed,
equation (
2.14
) which relates the tree paths
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}
immediately implies the bound

[\mathbf{Y}:\mathds{1}]_{I}\leq[\mathbf{Z}:\mathds{1}]_{I}+L^{1-N\alpha}\|Y\|_{I}^{m}\;.

Plugging this into the RHS of (
4.8
), the resulting terms involving
[\mathbf{Z}:\mathds{1}]_{I}
can be absorbed into the LHS, provided their pre-factors are small.
More precisely,

\displaystyle[\mathbf{Z}\colon\mathds{1}]_{I}

\displaystyle\lesssim L^{\alpha}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Bigg\{L^{|f|\alpha}U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)L^{1-N\alpha}\|Y\|_{I}^{m}+\max_{\bar{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|-1}}L^{(|\bar{f}|+|f|-N)\alpha}U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},I)[\mathbf{X}:\bar{f}]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{|f|\in\mathcal{F}_{=N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]\cdot\|\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{\bullet})\|_{I}\;,

provided for all forests
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
and all
\mu\in[d]

\displaystyle L^{(|f|+1)\alpha}\left[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}\right]U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)\leq\varepsilon\;,

for a suitably small
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\alpha,d,k)\leq 1
. This is the desired estimate (
4.9
).
∎

It turns out, that for the proof of Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
only the following truncated version of the estimate (
4.5
) is required

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
\mathbf{Y}
solve (
1.3
) on
[0,1]
in the sense of Definition
2.12
and let
\mathbf{Z}
be given by (
2.8
). Let
I\subseteq[0,1]
be a closed interval of length
L
satisfying (
4.4
).
Then we have the estimates

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Z]_{\alpha,I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\Bigg\{L^{(|f|+1)\alpha+1}U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)\|Y\|_{I}^{m}+\max_{\bar{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|-1}}L^{(|\bar{f}|+|f|+1)\alpha}U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},I)[\mathbf{X}:\bar{f}]\Bigg\}

(4.9)

\displaystyle+\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}L^{|h|\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:h]\|\Upsilon[h](Y_{\bullet})\|_{I}\,

and

(4.10)

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Y]_{\alpha,I}\lesssim\text{ RHS of \eqref{e:step3-final1}}+L\|Y\|_{I}^{m}\;.

Proof.

The estimate (
4.9
) follows from (
4.5
) combined with

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Z]_{\alpha,I}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}L^{|h|\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:h]\|\mathbf{Y}:h\|_{I}+L^{N\alpha}[\mathbf{Z}:\mathds{1}]_{I}\;,

as well as the identification

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{F}_{=N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}L^{N\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]\cdot\|\Upsilon_{\mu}[f](Y_{\bullet})\|_{I}=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{T}_{=N}}L^{|h|\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:h]\cdot\|\Upsilon[h](Y_{\bullet})\|_{I}\;.

The estimate (
4.10
) is an immediate consequence of the defining relationship
Y_{t}=y_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}|Y_{r}|^{m-1}Y_{r}dr+Z_{t}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
(1) - The case of bounded coefficients.

Step 1: Simplification of interior regularity estimate.

We now work under Assumption
1.1
, i.e. we assume that
\sigma
and its derivatives up to order
N
are bounded.
Using the boundedness of coefficients, and estimate (
4.1
) in Lemma
4.1
, this the estimate (
4.9
) simplifies to

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Z]_{\alpha,I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}L^{|h|\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:h]

\displaystyle+\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}L^{(|f|+1)\alpha+1}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]\|Y\|_{I}^{m}

(4.11)

\displaystyle+\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}\max_{\bar{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|-1}}L^{(|\bar{f}|+|f|+1)\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}][\mathbf{X}:\bar{f}]

provided
I
is an interval of length
L
satisfying

(4.12)

\displaystyle L^{(|f|+1)\alpha}\left[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}\right]\leq\varepsilon\;,

for all forests
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}
and for a potentially smaller value of
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\alpha,d,k,C_{\sigma})\leq 1
.
In order to simplify these expressions further, we make additional assumptions: first, we fix a
t^{\star}\in[0,1]

and for the remainder of this step we assume that the interval
I
satisfies
I\subseteq[t^{\star},1)
.
Next, we work under the assumption that for all trees
h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}
we have

(4.13)

[\mathbf{X}:h]\leq\varepsilon\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{|h|\delta\alpha}\;,

for
\delta=m
.
Under (
4.13
) the condition (
4.12
) is guaranteed as soon as

(4.14)

\displaystyle L\leq\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{-\delta}\;.

Under these assumptions the estimate (
4.11
)
simplifies further to

(4.15)

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Z]_{\alpha,I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon(1+L\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}+1+1)\lesssim\varepsilon+\varepsilon L\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}\;.

The estimate (
4.10
) turns into

(4.16)

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Y]_{\alpha,I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon+L\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}\;.

Step 2: Application of Lemma
1.11

We continue working under the boundedness Assumption
1.1
on the coefficients, and assuming the bound
(
4.13
) on the trees
[\mathbf{X}:h]
.
We apply Lemma
1.11
on the interval
[t_{\star},1]
and for

\displaystyle L=\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{-\delta}=\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{-m}\;.

We assume throughout that all times involved are contained in
[0,1]
which implies in particular that
L\in(0,1)
so that (
4.14
) implies

(4.17)

\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}\geq 1.

Under these assumptions, combining Lemma
1.11
with (
4.15
), (
4.16
) we obtain for
t>t_{\star}+L

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\lesssim\max\Big\{

\displaystyle(t-t^{\star}-L)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}};\Big(\varepsilon L^{-1}+\varepsilon\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}};

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m-1}\Big(\varepsilon+L\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}\Big)\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}};\Big(\varepsilon+L\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max\Big\{

\displaystyle(t-t^{\star}-L)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}};\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]};\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{\frac{m-1}{m}};1\Big\}\;.

We bound using Young’s inequality

\displaystyle\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{\frac{m-1}{m}}\leq\varepsilon\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}+C(\varepsilon,m)\;,

and then choose first
\varepsilon
small and absorb all constants into
the term
(t-t^{\star}-L)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\geq 1
and conclude that if
t
is large enough to ensure that

(4.18)

\displaystyle(t-t^{\star}-L)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\leq\bar{\varepsilon}\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]},

for
\bar{\varepsilon}=\bar{\varepsilon}(\alpha,d,k,C_{\sigma})
small enough, we get

(4.19)

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}.

We observe that (
4.18
) is guaranteed as soon as

\displaystyle t-t^{\star}\geq\bar{\varepsilon}^{-(m-1)}\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{-(m-1)}+\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{-m}.

which, taking into account (
4.17
) follows from the simpler condition

(4.20)

\displaystyle t-t^{\star}\geq(\bar{\varepsilon}^{-(m-1)}+1)\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{-(m-1)}.

Step 3: Coming down from infinity for bounded coefficients.

We continue working under the boundedness Assumption
1.1
and aim to establish
the estimate (
1.4
).
We define a finite sequence
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{N}=1
by setting

(4.21)

\displaystyle t_{n+1}-t_{n}=A\|Y\|_{[t_{n},1]}^{-(m-1)},

where
A=\bar{\varepsilon}^{-(m-1)}+1
(see (
4.20
)),
as long as the resulting
t_{n+1}
satisfies
t_{n+1}<1
. As soon as
t_{n+1}
defined by this rule is
\geq 1
we set

t_{n+1}=t_{N}=1
and terminate the sequence. Note that the sequence
t_{n+1}-t_{n}
is increasing by definition
(at least before passing
1
), so that this algorithm must terminate after a finite number of steps.

The time
t
is contained in precisely one interval
(t_{n},t_{n+1}]
and precisely one of the following must hold:

(1)

either there exists a
k\leq n
and a tree
h
such that

\varepsilon\|Y\|_{[t_{k},1]}^{|h|m\alpha}<[\mathbf{X}:h]\;,

(2)

or for all
k\leq n
all trees
h
satisfy condition (
4.13
) for
\delta=m
.

In case (1), the desired estimate (
1.4
) follows immediately from

|Y(t)|\leq\|Y\|_{[t_{k},1]}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{|h|m\alpha}}.

In case (2) invoking (
4.19
) repeatedly for
t_{*}=t_{k}
yields

(4.22)

\|Y\|_{[t_{n},1]}\leq 2^{k-n}\|Y\|_{[t_{k},1]}\qquad\text{ for }k=0,1,2,\ldots n.

We split (2) further into the following three cases:

(2a)

n+1<N
.

(2b)

n+1=N
and
t_{n}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

(2c)

n+1=N
and
t_{n}>\frac{1}{2}
.

In case (2a) we write using (
4.22
)

\displaystyle t_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}t_{k+1}-t_{k}=A\sum_{k=0}^{n}\|Y\|_{[t_{k},1]}^{-(m-1)}\leq A\|Y\|_{[t_{n},1]}^{-(m-1)}\sum_{k=0}^{n}2^{-(m-1)(n-k)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|Y\|_{[t_{n},1]}^{-(m-1)},

which implies

|Y(t)|\leq\|Y\|_{[t_{n},1]}\lesssim t_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\leq t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}},

as desired.

For (2b) to hold by definition (
4.21
) we have

A\|Y\|_{[t_{n},1]}^{-(m-1)}\geq\frac{1}{2},

and in particular

|Y(t)|\leq\|Y\|_{[t_{n},1]}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{2A}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}},

which implies (
1.4
).

Finally, in case (2c) we can apply case (2a) to
t_{n}
to get

|Y(t)|\leq\|Y\|_{[t_{n},1]}\leq t_{n}^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}},

completing the proof of (
1.4
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
(2) - The case of polynomial coefficients.

The proof follows the same general lines as the proof in the case of bounded coefficients and we only explain the differences.
Combining, their definition (
4.3
) with the estimate on
\Upsilon
provided by Lemma
4.1
,
yields

(4.23)

\begin{split}U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)&\lesssim\sup\big\{\langle Y_{s}+a\rangle^{(\gamma-1)(|f|+1)}:s,t\in I,|a|\leq E^{\mathbf{Y}}_{s,t}+|Y_{t}-Y_{s}|\big\}\;,\\
U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},I)&\lesssim\sup\bigg\{\langle Y_{s}+z\rangle^{(\gamma-1)(|f|+1)+1-\#\bar{f}}\langle Y_{s}\rangle^{(\gamma-1)|\bar{f}|+\#\bar{f}}:\begin{array}[]{c}s,t\in I,\\
|z|\leq E^{\mathbf{Y}}_{s,t}\end{array}\bigg\}\;.\end{split}

We follow the same approach as Step 1 of the proof in the case of bounded coefficients, fixing
t^{\star}\in[0,1)
, but here
the condition (
4.14
) is replaced by

(4.24)

\displaystyle L\leq\varepsilon_{2}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{-(m-1)}

where we have introduced an auxiliary parameter
\varepsilon_{2}\leq 1
that will ultimately be chosen small but still large relative to
\varepsilon
and we have changed values of exponents from
\delta=m
to
\delta=m-1
.
Instead of (
4.13
) we assume that for
h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}

(4.25)

[\mathbf{X}:h]\leq\varepsilon_{1}\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{|h|(\alpha(m-1)-(\gamma-1))}\;.

Note that the exponent
|h|(\alpha(m-1)-(\gamma-1))
appearing on the right hand side is positive if and only if the upper bound
\gamma<(m-1)\alpha+1
in Assumption
1.5
holds.
Under (
4.24
) and (
4.25
) we have

\displaystyle E^{\mathbf{Y}}_{s,t}=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}|\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle\Upsilon[h](Y_{s})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\Big(\varepsilon_{2}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{-(m-1)}\Big)^{\alpha|h|}\varepsilon_{1}\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{|h|(\alpha(m-1)-(\gamma-1))}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{(\gamma-1)|h|+1}

(4.26)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha}\varepsilon_{1}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}\;.

Note that in the first inequality, we have made use of (
4.2
) and the assumption
\gamma\geq 1
in (
1.5
) which ensures that the exponent
(\gamma-1)|h|+1
in the last term is non-negative
and permits to use the estimate

(4.27)

\displaystyle\Big|\Upsilon[h](Y_{s})\Big|\lesssim\langle Y_{s}\rangle^{(\gamma-1)|h|+1}\leq\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{(\gamma-1)|h|+1}\;.

We now enforce that

(4.28)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{C}\;,

Using this, (
4.26
), and once more the fact that
(\gamma-1)\geq 1
the estimate (
4.23
) simplifies to

\displaystyle U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},I)

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{(\gamma-1)(|f|+1)},

(4.29)

\displaystyle U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},I)

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{(\gamma-1)(|f|+\bar{f}+1)+1}\;.

Note that these estimates in conjunction with (
4.28
), (
4.24
), and Assumption (
4.25
) imply (
4.4
)
and in this case (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) turn into

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Z]_{\alpha,I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-1}}\sum_{\mu\in[d]}[\mathbf{X}:[f]_{\mu}]

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\Bigg\{L^{(|f|+1)\alpha+1}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{(|f|+1)(\gamma-1)}\|Y\|_{I}^{m}+\max_{\bar{f}\in\partial\mathcal{F}_{\leq N-|f|-1}}L^{(|\bar{f}|+|f|+1)\alpha}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{(|\bar{f}|+|f|+1)(\gamma-1)+1}[\mathbf{X}:\bar{f}]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}L^{|h|\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:h]\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{|h|(\gamma-1)+1}

(4.30)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon(L\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}+\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}+\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]})\lesssim\varepsilon\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}\;,

and

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Y]_{\alpha,I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}+L\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}\lesssim\varepsilon_{2}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m}\;.

As above in Step 2 we next apply Lemma
1.11
on the interval
[t_{\star},1]
yielding

\displaystyle\langle Y(t)\rangle\lesssim\max\Big\{

\displaystyle(t-t^{\star}-L)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}};\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]};

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\|Y\|_{[t^{\star},1]}^{m-1}\Big(\varepsilon_{2}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}\Big)\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}};\varepsilon_{2}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}\Big\}.

Then choosing first
\varepsilon_{2}
small to control the pre-factors in the second line and then also
\varepsilon
small to also make the pre-factors in the first line small (this amounts to choosing
\varepsilon_{1}
in (
4.25
) small)
we obtain

\displaystyle\langle Y(t)\rangle\leq\max\Big\{

\displaystyle C(t-t^{\star}-L)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}};\frac{1}{2}\langle Y\rangle_{[t^{\star},1]}\Big\}.

The proof concludes as in the case of bounded coefficients, Step 3 above, where in (
4.21
) and in what follows, the role of
\|Y\|_{[t_{k},1]}
is played by
\langle Y\rangle_{[t_{k},1]}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

We first show (
1.8
) under Assumption
1.1
(i.e. for bounded coefficients). We first define the auxiliary time

(4.31)

\displaystyle T_{0}=\inf\big\{t\in[0,1]\colon\langle y(t)\rangle\geq 3\langle y_{0}\rangle\big\}\;,

with the convention that
T_{0}=1
if the set is empty. Continuity of
|y(t)|
in
t
implies that if
T_{0}<1
we have

(4.32)

\displaystyle\langle y(T_{0})\rangle=3\langle y_{0}\rangle\;.

Furthermore, let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be as in (
1.7
), where
\varepsilon_{2}
is assumed to be small enough to play the role of
\varepsilon
in the absorption condition
(
4.12
) above and
\varepsilon_{1}
is fixed below, and set

T^{\star}=\min\{T_{0},T_{1},T_{2}\}\;.

Note that by choice of
T_{2}
condition (
4.12
) is automatically satisfied for
I=[0,T^{\star}]
and (
4.11
) turns into

\displaystyle(T^{\star})^{\alpha}[Z]_{\alpha,[0,T^{\star}]}\lesssim\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{1}3^{m}\langle y_{0}\rangle\;,

where we have used the fact that by definition

T^{\star}\langle Y\rangle_{[0,T^{\star}]}^{m}\leq\varepsilon_{1}\frac{1}{\langle y_{0}\rangle^{m-1}}3^{m}\langle y_{0}\rangle^{m}\leq\varepsilon_{1}3^{m}\langle y_{0}\rangle\;.

Hence we get from (
1.3
), choosing
\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{1}{2}3^{-m}
and possibly making
\varepsilon_{2}
smaller

\displaystyle\langle Y\rangle_{[0,T^{\star}]}\leq\|Y\|_{[0,T^{\star}]}+1

\displaystyle\leq|y_{0}|+T^{\star}\|Y\|_{[0,T^{\star}]}^{m}+(T^{\star})^{\alpha}[Z]_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq|y_{0}|+\varepsilon_{1}3^{m}\langle y_{0}\rangle+C\Big(\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{1}3^{m}\langle y_{0}\rangle\Big)

(4.33)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\langle y_{0}\rangle+1\leq\frac{5}{2}\langle y_{0}\rangle\;.

To conclude that (
1.8
) holds indeed, it remains to see that
T^{\star}=\min\{T_{0},T_{1},T_{2}\}=\min\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
. This is indeed the case, because otherwise we would
have
\|Y\|_{[0,T^{\star}]}=\|Y\|_{[0,T_{0}]}
and
T_{0}<1
, but then (
4.33
) would contradict (
4.32
).

We next pass to the case of polynomially bounded
\sigma
, i.e. to establishing (
1.10
) under Assumption
1.2
. Let
T_{0}
be as in (
4.31
) and
set

T^{\star}=\min\{T_{0},T_{1},T_{2}\}\;,

where this time
T_{2}
is defined in (
1.9
). We claim that on
I=[0,T^{\star}]
satisfies the Assumption (
4.4
). Indeed, just as in (
4.26
) above one can see that for
s,t
we have

\displaystyle E_{s,t}^{\mathbf{Y}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}|\langle\mathbf{X}_{s,t},h\rangle\Upsilon[h](Y_{s})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}(T^{\star})^{|h|\alpha}[\mathbf{X}:h]\|\Upsilon[h](Y_{\bullet})\|_{[0,T^{\star}]}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\varepsilon_{2}^{|h|\alpha}\frac{1}{[\mathbf{X}\colon h]}\Big(\frac{1}{\langle y_{0}\rangle}\Big)^{(\gamma-1)|h|}[\mathbf{X}:h]\langle Y\rangle_{[0,T^{\star}]}^{(\gamma-1)|h|+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N-1}}\varepsilon_{2}^{|h|\alpha}\frac{1}{[\mathbf{X}\colon h]}\Big(\frac{1}{\langle y_{0}\rangle}\Big)^{(\gamma-1)|h|}[\mathbf{X}:h]\langle Y\rangle_{[0,T^{\star}]}^{(\gamma-1)|h|+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha}\langle Y\rangle_{[0,T^{\star}]}\;,

where in the first inequality the fact
T^{\star}\leq T_{2}
and in the last inequality
T^{\star}\leq T_{0}
was used. From this one can conclude just as in (
4.29
) that

\displaystyle U_{\mu}(f,\mathbf{Y},[0,T^{\star}])

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle Y\rangle_{[0,T^{\star}]}^{(\gamma-1)(|f|+1)},

\displaystyle U_{\mu}(f,\bar{f},\mathbf{Y},[0,T^{\star}])

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle Y\rangle_{[0,T^{\star}]}^{(\gamma-1)(|f|+\bar{f}+1)+1}\;,

which in turn leads to

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}[Z]_{\alpha,[0,T^{\star}]}\lesssim\varepsilon\langle Y\rangle_{[0,T^{\star}]}\lesssim\varepsilon\langle y_{0}\rangle\,

where the first inequality follows like (
4.30
) and the second follows from
T^{\star}\leq T_{0}
. The rest of the argument follows as in the case of bounded coefficients.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.6
.

We treat the case of bounded coefficients first.
If we have
t\geq T_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}\frac{1}{\langle y_{0}\rangle^{m-1}}
or if
t\geq T_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{[\mathbf{X}\colon h]^{\frac{1}{|h|\alpha}}:h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}\bigg\}
defined in (
1.7
)
we use the estimate (
1.4
)

|Y(t)|\lesssim\max\Big\{t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\;,\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{m\alpha|h|}}\Big\}\;.

In the second term on the right-hand-side, we can replace the exponent
\frac{1}{m\alpha|h|}
by the slightly larger
\frac{1}{(m-1)\alpha|h|}
at the expense of adding the additional
1
into the maximum in (
1.11
).
For the first term, we use the lower bound on
t
provided by
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
. Indeed, for
t\geq T_{1}
we get

(4.34)

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\leq\varepsilon_{1}^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\langle y_{0}\rangle\;

and for
t\geq T_{2}
we have

t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\leq\varepsilon_{2}^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}\colon h]^{\frac{1}{(m-1)|h|\alpha}}\;

in each case establishing (
1.11
). Finally, if
t\leq\min\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
(
1.11
) follows from (
1.8
).

The argument for polynomial coefficients is very similar. If
t\geq T_{1}
or if
t\geq T_{2}
, where this time
T_{2}
is defined by (
1.9
), we use
the estimate (
1.6
)

\displaystyle|Y(t)|\lesssim\max\Big\{t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\;,\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{((m-1)\alpha-\gamma+1)|h|}}\Big\}\;.

For
t\geq T_{1}
(
4.34
) gives the desired (
1.12
), while for
t\geq T_{2}
we write

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{2}^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\langle y_{0}\rangle^{\frac{\gamma-1}{(m-1)\alpha}}\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}\colon h]^{\frac{1}{(m-1)|h|\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle y_{0}\rangle+\max_{h\in\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq N}}[\mathbf{X}:h]^{\frac{1}{((m-1)\alpha-\gamma+1)|h|}}\;,

where in the second inequality we have used the estimate
ab\leq a^{p}+b^{q}
for
p=\frac{(m-1)\alpha}{\gamma-1}
and
q=\frac{(m-1)\alpha}{(m-1)\alpha-\gamma+1}
.
As above, if
t\leq\min\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
(
1.12
) follows from (
1.10
).
∎
